- `seedSplit(id, T)` freezes ONE requirement's current lane draw as directed per-service dollars.
  One implementation, shared by the chip, the bulk convert and a newly added requirement.
- `convertAllToSplit()` moves a whole board over. It is a one-way bulk edit with **no undo** (the
  board has no undo stack), so it confirms with a count and points at Snapshots.
- `addReq()` seeds a new requirement on Split, because the menu can no longer offer auto.

**Conversion is lossless, and that is the property to protect.** Every dollar and both unfunded
readings are identical before and after — `tiers()` re-reads exactly what it just produced, and
unfunded is ask-based either way. `audit-board.mjs` asserts this across two fixtures, one with a
SCALAR trim so the per-service values are fractional: `seedSplit` rounds each service to whole
dollars, and whole-number fixtures would make that rounding invisible. If conversion ever shifts a
dollar it will do so across every requirement at once and silently.

### MIPR type, and the matrix column nobody could sweep

How an OGC's money is sent — **MIPR Direct Cite** or **MIPR Reimbursable**. The board records
*which*; how and when the money actually moves is decided outside it and is deliberately not
modelled. `S.mipr[id]` holds `'direct'` or `'reimb'`, `MIPR_LBL` is the only place either is
spelled out, and `setMipr()` refuses anything else — a free-text box here would produce eight
spellings of "MIPR Direct Cite" and a pivot table nobody can use, which is the same reasoning that
made `OB_CATS` a fixed list.

**The gate is `destIsOgc(it)`, keyed on the destination's KIND.** Not "LM or P&W" by name: a name
list goes stale the day a prime is renamed or a third is added, the same trap as the hardcoded year
list that blanked a whole appropriation in `spGather`. The audit asserts it by renaming a prime and
adding a third — a name list cannot survive either. The Goes-to completeness rule in BACKLOG entry 1
is the **same** test, so it must call this predicate rather than repeat it; a rule written out more
than once drifts, and this one already has three readers to keep honest.

**It is not on the Spend Plan Source export, and that is deliberate.** That export is a measured
mirror of a real workbook — its own comment says any change must come from re-measuring the file,
never from taste — and `QPE_READS` maps its columns **by index**, so inserting one would silently
re-point every column after it on the next import. MIPR type prints on the **🎯 Priority Drill**
instead. The record panel's footer used to read *"These fields are what the Spend Plan Source export
prints"*, which that made false; it now says *except where a field says otherwise*, and the MIPR
field carries the exception in its own note. A footer that is true of every field but one is worse
than no footer, because it is the one field you would check it for.

**The part that nearly shipped wrong.** `matCols()` derives the classification matrix's columns
from `recFields()`, so MIPR type became a matrix column on its own. But `matBulk()` is the one list
that is deliberately **not** derived — a bulk write needs the option values as data, not as rendered
markup — and a list-valued column with no bulk setter means the sweep covers less than the grid
draws. `matBulkOk()` is the board's own name for that gap. It returned `['MIPR type']` the moment
any requirement was routed to a government activity, and all twelve suites stayed green, because no
suite had ever called it on a board with a destination logged: **a check that cannot run reads as a
pass**, again. Two rules follow:

- **Any new record field rendering a `<select>` needs a `matBulk()` entry**, with its options read
  off the same constant the field reads, never retyped.
- The MIPR block in `audit-board.mjs` now asserts `matBulkOk()` is empty **on a routed board**, and
  that a bulk set writes the value rather than the label. Both fail when the setter is removed.

### A recorded funding source moves no money

`basisSvcOf()` used to return the full ask for a requirement carrying a directed funding source,
and the board allocation for every other. That made the same money carry two different names: the
gap between ask and allocation is TRIM on an ordinary requirement, but on one with a source
recorded against it the identical gap came out as BEYOND BOTH LANES — and beyond is what the
portfolio shortfall banner counts.

Because `seedSplit()` fills the directed dollars **from the current allocation**, the consequence
was that recording where money came from — moving no money whatsoever — flipped a requirement from
"fully funded" to "partly unfunded" and grew the banner. On a 115-requirement board that read as a
$38,325,128 portfolio shortfall which was entirely trim on the 7 requirements that happened to have
both, on a board whose lanes held $5.48B against $1.42B of asks and whose true lane shortfall was
**zero**. Two rows identical in ask, trim and funding sat side by side on the 1-N report reading
"fully funded" and "partly unfunded $4,671,122", with nothing on screen to say why.

The basis is the allocation now, for every requirement. A source says where the ALLOCATION is drawn
from; it is not a claim on the untrimmed ask. Everything else is unchanged: unfunded stays ask-based
(`beyond + trim == unfunded` still holds per service and in total), and the $107,017,782 the
workbook prints as TOTAL Unfunded did not move.

**The rule was written out three times** — `tiers()`, `basisSvcOf()` and `aOf()` in the workbook's
Unfunded Requirements sheet — so changing it in one place left the other two behind and the audit
caught the disagreement immediately. All three read `basisSvcOf()` now.

**`seedSplit()` no longer rounds.** It recorded the lane draw rounded to whole dollars, which was
invisible while the basis was the ask (the rounded figure sat well under that ceiling and passed
through untouched). Against the allocation, a value rounded DOWN loses its fraction to unfunded and
one rounded UP is capped — so converting a board to Split stopped being lossless, by cents, on
every requirement at once. `audit-board.mjs` catches it through the fractional-trim fixture, which
exists for exactly this.

Two guards hold the property: `a recorded funding source moves no money` in `audit-board.mjs` (two
copies of one requirement, differing only in whether a source is recorded, asserted equal on
discretionary, mandatory, beyond, trim, unfunded, basis and tier), and the `basis-fsrc` mutation,
inverted to put the old ask-basis back.

**EVERY per-service figure is floored PER SERVICE, not once on the totals.** A "service" is a
service AND ITS VARIANT — STOVL, USMC CV and USN are each their own pot — and money never moves
between them, so an over-allocation in one can never pay down a shortage in another. This governs
the MC shortage and unfunded alike. The two readings agree on any board with no over-allocation,
which is why only a deliberately over-allocated scenario pins it down.

`unfSvcOf()`, `beyondSvcOf()` and `trimSvcOf()` are the single implementation, defined next to
`mcCalc()`. Both exports and the Decision Bands sheet read them. Defined so that, per service and
in total, `beyond + trim == unfunded` always — including an over-allocated service, where the trim
is nothing and the lane shortfall is capped at the ask.

One consequence worth knowing: where a service is funded beyond its ask, `funded + unfunded`
exceeds the requirement. That excess is real money and does not vanish. The identity that holds is
`funded + unfunded == max(ask, funded)`, per service.

A starred requirement carried entirely by the mandatory lane is **fully short**. A starred
requirement trimmed on the board is short by the trim, even if discretionary covered the trimmed
amount in full. `tiers()` draws discretionary before it touches mandatory, so `p.d` is already the
discretionary-only answer — there is no need for a second walk (that is what `{noMand:true}` and
`scenView==='disc'` exist for elsewhere).

Exports carry two MC memo figures: **★ MC requirement** (the starred requirements' own value) and
**★ MC short vs Disc** (the gap that drives the ask). Neither is ever added into a lane total.

**"By PMO" means one thing everywhere.** `pmoSharesOf()` returns `[group, fracPerService]`; a
CLIN-backed prime is split across the offices owning its CLINs. Weight **per service**
(`x[i] * frac[i]`) — never by an average across services. Every by-PMO view in the workbook uses
this, so the tabs reconcile against each other.

`GROUPS` is the *configured* PMO list and is not the whole truth: `pmoSharesOf` can derive `PRIMES`
from a CLIN suffix, and a PMO removed from the list leaves its requirements behind. Never iterate
`GROUPS` alone to emit rows — use `pmoKeys()`, which puts the configured offices first and appends
anything else rather than losing it.

A requirement split across offices is counted in **each** row it touches, so a by-PMO count column
must total the requirements on the board, not the sum of its own rows — otherwise a prime split
three ways reads as three requirements.

One implementation each, all hoisted beside `mcCalc()` so the workbook, the deck and the board
cannot diverge: `pmoKeys()`, `mcShortSvcOf()`, `unfSvcOf()`, `beyondSvcOf()`, `trimSvcOf()`.

### The ◨/◧ view toggle — discretionary only vs discretionary + mandatory

The board can be read two ways, and the toggle switches between them **for the spend-plan family
only**. `scenView` is the state (`''` or `'disc'`); `scenOpt()` returns `{noMand:true}` under
"Discretionary only", which runs the ordinary lane walk with **every mandatory lane empty** — so
what would have spilled into mandatory shows as unfunded and never reaches a month. There is no
second engine: it is `tiers()` with one option.

**What follows the toggle** — the SPEND-PLAN FAMILY, every surface that states WHEN funded dollars
land, and nothing else:

| Surface | How |
|---|---|
| RCN-EEIC by Month (sheet) | `const Tv = scenOn()? tiers(null, scenOpt()) : T` |
| SI Codes by Month (sheet) | the same `Tv` |
| CR Cash Needs (sheet) | `crData(scenOpt())` |
| CR cash panel (on screen) | `crData(scenOpt())` |
| Spend forecast **tab** (on screen) | `forecast()` bare: `const T = fundOf ? null : tiers(null, scenOpt())` |

**The 1A9A CR Cash Return is the one spend-plan surface that does NOT follow it**, and the exception
is asserted, not just commented. It is a formal external submission on a fixed format — header row
`FY<yy> CRA REQUIREMENTS` — with nowhere to stamp `[DISC-ONLY]` without corrupting what the
recipient parses. A what-if that cannot be labelled a what-if is precisely how model figures end up
under a real heading. `crExportRows()` therefore calls `crData()` with no scenario, and the suite
compares its rows across both views.

The workbook sheets are safe to move because the WHOLE workbook filename carries `[DISC-ONLY]`
(`scenFile`, line 20779) and each family sheet stamps `scenTitle()` on its own title row.

**What must NOT follow it:** every other sheet in the workbook, the deck, and the board's own
totals. Unfunded Requirements is ask-versus-allocation rather than a lane shortfall, so the toggle
would misstate it.

> **A "differs under the toggle" check can be satisfied by its own marker.** These sheets stamp
> `scenTitle()` on their title row, so comparing a serialized sheet BODY across the two views
> differs whether or not a single dollar moved. A CR sheet deliberately wired to ignore the toggle
> still "passed" that check. The family checks compare the sheets' NUMBERS with every label
> stripped (`look().nums`), plus an assertion that the sheet carries figures at all — two empty
> grids agree and prove nothing. Whole-body comparison still has a job: it proves a sheet did NOT
> move. It can never prove one did. The audit compares **every sheet** between the two views, not just one — the
month sheets are built partway through `exportExcel`, so a view leaking into the shared `T` would
corrupt only the sheets built *after* them and a single-sheet check sails straight past it.

**The `<FY> Two-Plan Forecast` SHEET is deliberately on the invariant side.** It is not the board's
forecast: it is PLAN A (discretionary only) and PLAN B (disc + mandatory) printed side by side, both
modelled through `fundOf`. It already shows both answers at once, so the toggle must not move it.
The on-screen forecast tab is the one that follows the view. These are different objects — do not
"fix" the sheet to match the tab.

The sheet was called `<FY> Spend Forecast` until it was renamed for exactly that reason: one word
away from the tab, and readers compared a two-plan model against one set of allocations. The audit
matches it on `/Forecast/` and **fails when it is missing** rather than skipping — the old
`if (fc)` meant a rename would have quietly disabled every check on the sheet while the suite still
read green.

**The marker is part of the behaviour, not decoration.** A workbook built under "Discretionary
only" is a WHAT-IF, and stops being obviously one the moment it is emailed. `scenTitle()` stamps
the month-sheet titles, `scenFile()` appends `[DISC-ONLY]` to the file name, and `fcBody()` marks
the on-screen forecast panel. Each is asserted in **both** directions — present under the toggle,
absent without it — the two exported ones in `audit.mjs`, the on-screen one in `audit-board.mjs`,
because it is rendered and never exported and no export suite can reach it. Never ship a figure
that moves with the view without a marker saying so.

### Auditing the exports

```
npm install        # jsdom, for the audit only
npm run audit          # every suite
npm run audit:export   # the Excel workbook (exportExcel)
npm run audit:other    # the Priority Drill (exportCSV), and more
npm run audit:board    # figures the board derives and never exports
```

Point any of them at the grid build with `BOARD=$PWD/prioritization-board-grid.html`.

**Which exports do lane math at all.** Two do it directly since v2.05 removed the deck and the synopsis: `exportExcel`
and `exportCSV` (it was four, with `exportPPT` and `execSynopsis`). ACE Tie-Out, Chart of Accounts, ATR/BTR Log and CR Cash Return reconcile against
imported sources and never touch `tiers()`, the star or unfunded, so they stay outside these suites.

**The month-by-month sheets are the exception, and they are easy to miss.** *Spend Plan Source* is
an IMPORTED workbook, not an export — do not go looking for it here. What spreads `tiers()` output
across months is:

- `monSpreadOf` → the **RCN-EEIC by Month** and **SI Codes by Month** sheets
- `forecast()` → the on-screen spend forecast tab, and the **<FY> Two-Plan Forecast** sheet (two modelled plans)

**Those are two copies of the same spread and they must not drift.** `forecast()`'s own comment says
so, and they drifted anyway the moment one was changed alone. Change one, change the other.

They differ in exactly one respect, and it is deliberate. An increment is DOLLARS only against the
board's own allocation: `forecast()` bare and `monSpreadOf` spread what the board actually funds, so
what was typed is what lands. `forecast(fundOf)` spreads a MODELLED plan whose dollars are not the
ones the increments were sized against, so there the increments carry only the SHAPE of the
schedule. Spreading a model literally strands most of it in UNSCHEDULED.

Both carry a conservation property: every funded dollar lands in a month or in UNSCHEDULED, never
dropped and never invented. All of it is asserted in `audit-board.mjs` rather than the export
suites, because this is board logic that exports happen to consume.

**The suite's epsilon is 0.005 DOLLARS.** The Two-Plan Forecast sheet prints in $M, where that tolerance
is enormous: a whole dropped PMO came to 0.000012 and passed. Scale to dollars before comparing
anything printed in millions.

`tools/export-audit/` boots the real board in jsdom, seeds boards with deliberately uneven
per-service numbers, runs the real `exportExcel()` and `exportPPT()`, and checks every printed
figure against `tiers()`. Add a scenario there before changing export math. The board itself still
has **no** dependencies — jsdom is dev tooling and never ships in the HTML.

### The nightly IA review — how it looks, and where things are filed

```
npm run ia:census     # structure: menus, groups, items, hints, positions, duplicates, orphans
npm run ia:shots      # 16 fixed views into .ia-shots/
npm run ia            # both
```

The **SPMT Interface Review** Routine (the Routine keeps the name it was created under) fires at **08:00 UTC** (an hour after the math run, so they do
not compete for the container) and reviews the board's visuals and information architecture: menu
headers, dropdown grouping, where clickable items sit and how they are categorised. It also runs a
**market scan** — a weekday-rotating look at what named commercial tools and published design
systems do (navigation Monday, data density Tuesday, filters Wednesday, exports Thursday, summary
tiles Friday, editing Saturday, colour and accessibility Sunday). It is **advisory, not a gate** —
it proposes, a person decides, and it is forbidden from changing code.

> **The network constraint, verified 15 Sep 2026.** `WebSearch` works. `WebFetch` and curl to any
> external host are **blocked by the egress proxy** (`EGRESS_BLOCKED`, organization policy). So the
> scan's evidence is SEARCH-RESULT SUMMARIES, not pages read in full — weaker evidence, and the
> report is required to say so rather than write "according to Tableau's documentation" for
> something seen only in a snippet. The Routine carries this fact so no night is spent
> rediscovering it. It is also told that a scan returning only marketing listicles should be
> reported as having found nothing usable: a confidently wrong claim about a competitor's interface
> is worse than no scan at all, because it is indistinguishable from a real one and it will be
> acted on.

**The tools state facts; the agent forms the opinion, and the report keeps the two apart.** The
census measures and never advises — a census that editorialises is one you cannot check. The nightly
report is written in three separated parts:

| Part | What belongs in it |
|---|---|
| OBSERVED | facts only, each with its number or the screenshot it came from |
| ASSUMPTIONS | everything inferred to interpret those facts — who the user is, how often an item is used, what a group name promises |
| RECOMMENDATIONS | what to change, the evidence, what improves, the risk, and a feasibility read |

The ASSUMPTIONS section is the part that makes the rest usable. A recommendation is only worth
acting on if the reader can see which parts were measured and which were guessed, and an assumption
nobody wrote down is one nobody can correct. The report is also told that **"no change" is a
legitimate finding** — a review that invents work to look busy is worse than no review.

**Two measurement notes, both learned by getting them wrong first.** A menu item is
`<span.chk></span> LABEL <span.sub>hint</span>`: taking `innerText` and splitting on newlines runs
label and hint together, which reported every item as having no hint while the hint sat inside the
label. And **every** item carries a `.chk` span — only a state-bearing one that is currently ON has
a tick in it (measured: 542 empty, 4 ticked), so the census records "shows a tick right now", which
is a fact, and does not claim to identify toggles: an OFF toggle and a one-shot action are identical
in the DOM, and a census must not guess at what it cannot see.

The shot set is **fixed on purpose**. A nightly review is only useful if last night and tonight are
comparable, so the views do not vary with whatever happens to be open. `closeAll()` resets state
between captures for the reason `panels-render.mjs` already documents: removing a panel's class
without clearing its flag lets `render()` put it straight back, and the next screenshot then shows
the previous view's panel.

> A wrong selector here is silent — `snap()` records a MISS rather than throwing. That is what caught
> `#scoreboard` not existing (it is `#scores`), and the same wrong id had been sitting in
> `trace-matrix.mjs` falling back to `document.body`. The regexes there were specific enough that it
> still read the right figures, but a selector that silently matches everything is one edit away from
> reading the wrong one. It is pinned now and throws if the scoreboard moves.

### The trace matrix — one quantity, every surface that states it

```
npm run trace
```

The same dollars travel from the requirement math to a dashboard tile, a panel, a workbook column, a
band subtotal and a slide. A reader can only trust any of them if they agree, and until this existed
the only way to find a disagreement was for someone to compare a deck against a workbook in a
meeting. The matrix cross-foots **10 surfaces × 12 quantities — 60 populated cells** against a truth
computed from the fixture's own numbers, never read back out of the board.

Quantities: ask · discretionary BA and drawn · mandatory BA and drawn · funded · **both** unfunded
readings · trim · beyond lanes · MC value · MC short. Surfaces: the lane engine, the rendered
dashboard (read off the DOM, because a figure computed right and displayed wrong is still wrong on
the screen someone is briefing from), Funding Streams, Decision Bands, the PMO Matrix, Unfunded
Requirements, the Priority Drill's band subtotals, the Spend Plan Source, and both deck tables.

**A cell is one of three things, and the third is the point:**

| | |
|---|---|
| `ok` | the surface states it and agrees |
| `MISMATCH` | it states it and does NOT agree — the run fails and names quantity, surface, got, want, delta |
| `--` | the surface does not state it. **Not an error.** A slide that never mentions trim is silent, not wrong, and silence is recorded rather than scored |

Two rules keep it honest. **Every cell is located by NAME** — a column header, a row label — never by
index; a lookup that silently re-points is the exact failure this file exists to catch, so it must
not be the failure of the file itself. And **units are normalised, not ignored**: the deck abbreviates
to `$M`/`$B`, so it is compared at ±$60k — the rounding the format imposes, not a licence to drift.
The deck printing `$33.0M` against a truth of `$32,950,000` is correct; the same cell printing
`$28.0M` is not.

Proven to bite: point the Decision Bands summary at reading #2 where reading #1 belongs and it
reports *"xl · Decision Bands says $10,750,000, the board says $15,950,000 (off by $-5,200,000)"* —
and that delta is the mandatory draw, which names the cause as well as the location.

Two quantities the matrix deliberately does NOT map: the Unfunded Requirements sheet's own ask and
allocated columns. They cover only the LISTED requirements, so they are not board-wide and comparing
them to a portfolio total would fail for the wrong reason. Its two unfunded totals ARE board-wide —
every requirement short on either reading is listed — which is what makes those comparable.

### The funds flow's plan year is the lane draw

On a fiscal year with no execution import, the Funds Flow is answered from the board. It was
answered from the **allocation** — `svcAlloc()`, unclamped — under a comment claiming that matched
the Spend Plan Source so the two could not disagree. `SRC_BASIS` is `'disc'`; they already did.
Measured on the reconciliation fixture: the flow's TOTAL FUNDING read **$29,752,779** while every
other surface read Funded as **$23,300,000** — the $6,452,779 beyond both lanes counted as funding —
and with one requirement allocated at twice its ask the flow rose to $34,627,779, $4.9M above the
total requirement. A funds flow above the funded topline is exactly the figure that draws concern
against the board. The basis is now the lane draw (`tiers()` d+m per service), which is what every
other surface calls Funded. Registered as `flow-plan-year-printed-the-allocation`; guarded by *the
funds flow* in `audit-recon.mjs` and five `flow-*` mutations. Found by closing a coverage gap, which
is what the gaps are recorded for.

### PPBE cycles: a lock is the FYDP window, whole, in its own store

A cycle (`PB|2027` reads PB27) is every year in the FYDP window that held a plan, copied whole
under one key, plus a per-year projection — the shape the one-year baselines had — for the
compare and the Spruills. `CYCDB` is its own store beside `EXDB` and `ACEDB`, per appropriation
(`_AP.key+'-cyc'`), packed into the file as `cycDb.byAp` the way `appns` packs the boards, and
merged on import the way an approved estimate is: a key not held is taken, a key held is left
alone and said. The working plan carries `MS.cycle` (its tag) and `MS.cycleRef` (the lock its
deltas are read against); locking an untagged plan tags it, because it *is* the plan just locked.
`cycWindowYears()` is the lock's scope: `base`…`base+4` plus the year on screen, each only where
`MS.years` holds it — a blank year is recorded as *no plan*, never as an empty one. The projection
is taken per year through `aceWithYear`, so FY28's projection carries FY28's values, and the copy
is taken *after* it so the stored year is the normalised object `applyState` hands back. `fund` in
a projection is the lane draw (d+m) and `alloc` the allocation, kept apart on purpose — the flow
already paid once for confusing them. One-year baselines from before migrate as `partial` cycles
and the legacy container is deleted once carried, or a cycle deleted on purpose would come back on
the next load. Guarded by *PPBE cycles — the lock is the window, frozen* in `audit-board.mjs`,
thirteen `cycle-*` mutations, and a trace-matrix surface that reads Funded back out of the lock.

### Viewing a locked cycle: the store is set aside, a throwaway copy goes on the page

`cycView(key)` stashes the whole working store (`MS`, `S`, the dirty flag, the title) and puts a
deep copy of it on the page with the cycle's years in it, then applies the year through the same
`applyState` every year goes through. There is no second renderer: every panel, export and helper
reads the lock because the lock *is* what `MS.years` holds. Two consequences are load-bearing. A
write cannot reach anything durable because the working plan is not on the page — and every write
funnels through `save()` or `persist()`, which refuse and put the pristine year back
(`cycReapply`), so a refused edit never sits on screen looking kept; `setStat` keeps the refusal
in front of the handler's own message for a second and a half. And `cycLeave()` restores the
stashed objects themselves, so the working plan comes back byte for byte (the suite asserts it).
The gates that must also refuse: `buildSavedHtml` (throws — an empty string would be written as
the file), every outbound function beside `sandboxBlock`, `applyStore` (a load over a view would
lose the stash), `cycLock`, `cycSetWorking`, the year-level mutators and `svcSaveList` (which
writes `MS.svc`), and `liveTick`, which would otherwise push the lock as the live board.
`switchYear` stays inside the cycle and refuses a year it never held rather than rolling one
forward. `CYCVIEW` and its two companions are declared beside `let MS`, not with the cycle code:
`save()`, `persist()`, `setStat()` and `applyStore()` read them, `applyStore()` runs at top level,
and a `let` further down leaves them in the temporal dead zone — the BANDS_STD rule, and the first
cut threw on boot and rendered nothing. The trace harness syncs `BASE_ITEMS` after `seed()` for the
same reason every suite does: the view applies state, and the board rebuilds from that baseline. The banner, the header tag, `document.title` (which several exports print) and
`expName` (every file name) all say which lock is on screen. Guarded by *PPBE cycles — a locked
view is read-only and leaves no trace*, fifteen `view-*` mutations, and a trace-matrix surface
that reads all twelve quantities off the view and expects the engine's.

### Verifying the file you brief from

Everything else here proves the math on fixtures. `npm run verify:file -- <saved-board.html>`
(`tools/verify-file.mjs`) opens the board someone actually briefs from, in the harness, and prints
a certificate: **LOADS** (the state was adopted, not defaulted or refused; nothing reported
unreadable; every id in the order resolves; every per-service vector has one figure per service;
each side store came back whole; the year on screen came back whole, with normalisation reported
apart from loss; a census of populated and empty containers), **SURVIVES** (save → reopen returns
every value, the side stores travel byte for byte, a second save is stable), **TIES** (the lane
identities per requirement per service, then the trace-matrix harvests from `trace-lib.mjs` held
to the engine — on real data there is no fixture truth, so every surface is held to `tiers()` and
the identities hold `tiers()` to itself), **CROSS-FOOTS** (the four summary sheets' total rows
against the rows above them, every per-service breakout against its figure, every other workbook
builds), and, when the working tree is a different build from the one that wrote the file,
everything again **under the current build** with the same data injected, and **MOVED** — every
figure addressed by sheet, row label and column header, diffed across the two builds. Every slot
with a plan in it is examined (the boot is told which appropriation to open). Exit 1 on any FAIL;
MOVED is a report. **The file is read, never copied into the repo**: keep it in the scratch folder
or a temp directory; `.gitignore` refuses every other `.html` anyway. `--selftest` builds a
synthetic saved file, certifies it, plants a ghost in the order and truncates the state, and must
notice both — a verifier that cannot fail is a rumour — and that self-test is a stage of
`validate:math`. The lock-and-view harvests run under a scratch cycle (`POM|2999`, deleted after)
because on a real file `PB|<year>` may be a real lock taken weeks ago.

### Deltas against the reference cycle: the same two definitions, subtracted

`cycDelta(fy)` compares the year on screen to the reference cycle's projection of the same year
(`MS.cycleRef`, set by the lock), requirement by requirement, per service, then summed. 'Now' is
`tiers()` for funded and `svcAlloc()` for the allocation — exactly what `cycProjOf` wrote into
the lock — so a row's delta is one definition less itself a lock ago, never a second computation.
It reads only the year on screen (a delta for another year would need that year on the board),
is computed once per render (`_cycDeltaCache`, cleared at the top of `render()`), and reports
rank (positive = moved up), NEW (an id the lock never held), REMOVED (a lock id the order no
longer holds, with its locked figures), band, PMO and star changes. The totals are accounted for
by the rows: changed rows plus new, less removed, equals the total change, and the panel prints
that identity with a ✓ or ✗ so a reader can see it close. Who and when come from the journal —
entries newer than the lock that name the requirement — and the priority order is not journaled
(`JRNL_SKIP`), so a pure rank change says so rather than inventing a name. The chip is one line
under the row in both builds; the grid's record panel shows was → now; the Changes panel is under
🎯 Budget year. After a lock the status offers the retag: PB27 is followed by BES28, POM by BES
the same year, BES by PB. Guarded by *PPBE cycles — the deltas against the reference* (a lock
holding two years with the base on the second, so a delta read against the wrong year cannot pass
by coincidence), thirteen `delta-*`/`retag-*`/`who-*` mutations, and a trace-matrix surface that
reads the panel's 'Now' side and expects the engine.

### The five-year spend plan: one shape for both sources

`fydpData(src)` returns the same shape for the working plan and for a locked cycle: the years of
the window (`fydpWindow()` — `base`…`base+4` plus the year on screen, the span a lock takes), the
years actually held, and a projection per held year. The working plan is projected through
`aceWithYear(y, cycProjOf)`, the very function a lock stores, so a working-plan report and a
locked-cycle report are the same figures taken at different times, never two computations. A locked
cycle's report reads its projection **as locked**: recomputing a lock with today's code would
quietly restate a certified position, which is the one thing a lock exists to prevent. Funded is
the lane draw (d + m); Allocated is `svcAlloc` (the draw itself for a funding-source row);
Unfunded is requirement less funded, floored per service — the reading the board calls TOTAL
unfunded — and never netted across services. A year the source never held prints an em dash in
the workbook and on the panel, never 0, and stays outside the FYDP totals. The workbook (*📊 FYDP
Spend Plan (Excel)*; *ORBIT FYDP Spend Plan — PB27 — FY27–FY31 date.xlsx*, with the `[PB27
LOCKED]` mark when a locked view is on screen) carries By Requirement (one row per requirement
with PMO, CCaR and band, `Subtotal — <PMO>`, TOTAL), By PMO (the subtotals, with a count) and By
Service (the projection's vectors added, Disc BA and Mand BA beside the draw); the panel (*📊
Five-year spend plan…* under FYDP Views) shows the PMO subtotals and the total for a source
picked from the working plan and every locked cycle that holds a year. On the panel a year is a
block (v1.79, asked for from the real board): each year's three columns start at a 2px rule in
`--edge` with the year centred over them, the PMO column is pinned left and never wraps, and the
cell markup is unchanged because the recon suite reads the table by its cells. Guarded by *the five-year
spend plan* in audit-recon (the year on screen against `boardFigures`, a second held year against
the engine under a year swap, blank years, subtotals, TOTAL, By PMO, By Service, the panel, then a
PB lock and a moved value the locked report must not follow), fourteen `fydp-*` mutations, and the
trace-matrix surface *xl · FYDP Spend Plan* (requirement, funded, TOTAL unfunded, both pots).

### Comparing two cycles: the same shape twice, and the rows account for the totals

`cycCmpData(a, b)` reads both sources through `fydpData` — a locked cycle as it was locked, the
working plan as it stands — so A and B are the same figures taken at different times, never two
computations. Per year both hold, per requirement: A, B and Δ = B − A for requirement, allocated,
funded (the lane draw) and unfunded, with rank, band, PMO and the star beside them; a requirement
in B and not in A is ADDED, one in A and not in B is REMOVED, and each carries its whole figure as
Δ. That is what makes the conservation rule hold — **Σ row deltas = the total delta, per year, per
column** — and the rule is computed (`closes`), printed on the panel and on both sheets (a CHECK
row), and asserted by the recon suite from the engine before and after the changes. For the year
on screen the comparison must agree with `cycDelta` (stage 3): two paths, one answer. A year only
one source holds is stated (*FY29 — not in PB27*) and carries no figures; the same source twice is
refused. The workbook (*⇄ Cycle Comparison (Excel)*; *ORBIT Cycle Comparison — PB27 vs WORKING —
FY27–FY28 date.xlsx*) carries Compare (every requirement, PMO subtotals, TOTAL, CHECK), Changes
(per year: added, removed and changed rows with what changed, Σ ROWS, TOTAL Δ, CHECK, the pots) and
By PMO. The panel (*⇄ Compare two cycles…* under FYDP Views; the cycles panel's ⇄ Compare opens it
with that cycle as A) shows per year the totals as A, B, Δ, the pots, the ✓ line and the changed
rows. The old one-year "compare with the board now" in the cycles panel is gone: it was a third
computation of the same figures. Guarded by *comparing two cycles* in audit-recon, fifteen `cmp-*`
mutations, and the trace-matrix surface *xl · Cycle Comparison* (the lock just taken against the
working plan: every Δ zero, and the B side the engine).

### The PPBE position beside the plan, and what a PMO copy carries

The By Service sheet of the FYDP spend plan prints, per held year and for the FYDP, the official
PPBE position logged under the plan's cycle key (`fydpPosKey`: the working plan's tag, or the
locked cycle's own key) in 🏛 PPBE Budget Positions, by service, and *Requirement − position*.
It is read live from `MS.ppbe` — a lock never copies it, because the position is the comptroller's
figure and not the board's — and a service or year with nothing logged prints an em dash, never
0 (`fydpPos` returns null unless a cell is logged for that year); the FYDP position is printed
only when every held year logs one; a working plan with no cycle tag prints none and the note says
so. The panel prints the same per held year. Guarded by *the PPBE position beside the plan*
(audit-recon: typed positions re-derived per service and year, the gap, TOTAL, blank years and
services, the locked report after a retag, the untagged plan) and seven `fydp-position-*` /
`fydp-panel-position-*` mutations.

A PMO copy carries **one** cycle — the reference the working plan's deltas are read against — as
a projection only (`cycRefOnlyDb`: `years:{}`, `partial:true`, `refOnly:true`), under the copy's
own appropriation, and nothing else of the store: no year states (size), no other lock (not the
office's to see), nothing that can be put on the drill (`cycView` refuses a cycle with no years).
The copy's rows still show Δ, its compare and its five-year plan still read the cycle
(`cycReportable` lists every cycle that holds a projection; `cycHeld` stays the test for the
drill), and a lock taken on the copy goes into the copy's own store. `pmoCopyHtml(mode)` is the
one builder (`buildSavedHtml({refOnly:true})`), used by `publishPmoCopy` and by the check. Guarded
by *PPBE cycles — a PMO copy carries the reference projection only* (audit-board: the store in the
file, the copy booted, its deltas, plan and compare, the refused view, the size) and four `copy-*`
mutations.

### A suite that dies is not a suite that caught something

`mutate.mjs` counted every non-zero exit as a catch. Under heavy load (a gate's two sweeps beside
a worktree's runs) the same *equivalent* mutation was "caught (board)" on two sweeps, and passed
both times the board suite was run on it by hand: a jsdom worker had died, and a death exits
non-zero exactly as a failed check does. On a non-equivalent mutation that would have been
invisible — a crash reading as coverage. A deliberate failure prints its banner (`N FAILING
CHECK(S)`, or recon's *the workbook did not build*); a death does not. `runSuite` now reads the
banner, a death is retried once and then reported as **CRASHED** with the suite and the last lines
it wrote, the summary lists them, and a crash fails the sweep and the gate's sweep stage the way
NOT APPLIED does. The nightly report's rule stands: a mutation without a verdict proved nothing.

The first gate run with the hardened runner (v1.75, 21 Sep 2026) reported **thirteen CRASHED** on
each build — every one a stage-1 or stage-2 cycle mutation (`cycle-*`, `view-load-allowed`) that
the earlier gates had counted as *caught* only because the board suite died on it: a check read
`.reqs` off a projection the mutation had removed, or the page threw inside `ev`. Nothing was
wrong with the board; the suite had no verdict. The second run (v1.76) found five more of the
same kind in other blocks (`mipr-no-bulk-setter`, `fld-stale-stays-open`, `disc-mand-gated` in
the board suite; `save-no-lt-escape`, `svc-check-order-blind` in the import suite). So **every
scenario block** of audit-board, audit-import and audit-recon — a top-level `{ const name = … }`
— now wraps its body in `try { … } catch (e) { failures.push({ … 'the block threw: ' + message
… }) }` (a script did it; the four cycle blocks were first), so a throw inside a block is a
FAILING CHECK naming the block and the error, and only a death outside any block — boot, a
syntax error, the process killed — remains a crash. The runner's crash report now keeps the error
message (Node prints it above the stack, which the last lines alone lost; and `TypeError` has no
word boundary before `Error`, which the first regex assumed).

### The five money-coverage gaps are closed

Each closed the same way: a check that derives the figure from the fixture (never from the code
under test), a mutation inside the surface — or a `via` helper named in the register — that the
check catches, and the `gap:` line struck. `crData` (six placement mutations, on a fixture built
for it: a flipped half, weighted increments with one outside Oct–Mar, no date, no weight, one
service only), `exportFlow` (five), `exportCoa` (two, on a year override the payload does not
carry — and on a consolidated Full Fund slot, because on the board's own appropriation
`applyState` has already written the override into the live item, so a `coaAsk()` that ignores the
override changes nothing there; the mutation survived until the fixture stood up the slot the
branch exists for), `exportReprog` (four, deducts negative and types apart), `exportAceTieout` (four). Closing
the tie-out found a second defect: a confirmed estimate line under a PMO no office aligns to was
counted on one sheet and dropped from the other — registered as
`ace-estimate-line-nobody-reports-under-vanished`; the sheet now states such lines and keeps them
out of its total.

### A block after `process.exit()` never runs, and reads as a pass

The proposal scenario was appended to the end of `audit-import.mjs` — after the summary and its
`process.exit(1)`. It never executed. The import suite reported ALL CHECKS PASSED on a board with
the proposal deliberately dropped, and five mutations aimed at the scenario survived; that was the
only signal. Every suite now asserts, at its summary, that every `const name = '…'` in its own
source was heard from by `chk()`; one that was not fails as *never ran*. The same trap as a fixture
that cannot reach its target, one level up: a check that cannot be reached at all.

Proven to bite: moving *the two spend plans — the A vs B tab* after `process.exit(1)` in a scratch
copy of `audit-other.mjs` reports *"this scenario never ran — is its block after process.exit()?"*
by name. A name built by concatenation (`'convert to split — ' + label`) is heard only in its
finished forms, so a literal that any heard scenario starts with counts as run; the first cut
flagged that one falsely. And the first version of the proof moved the self-check along with the
scenario, because the cut stopped at the last `}` before the summary — which is the self-check's
own. A proof that cannot reach its target reads as a pass too.

### A submittal can carry a new requirement, whole

A requirement added on a PMO copy has no id on the portfolio board, so it cannot travel as field
deltas the way an edit does. It travels **whole**, in the package's own `added` section, flagged
as new, and the portfolio team accepts or declines it as one thing. Three rules, each guarded:

- **Gated in the copy, not at the far end.** `pmoProposalGate()` names every mandatory field still
  blank; `pmoSubmit()` withholds that proposal *by name* and sends everything else the office
  edited — the first cut blocked the whole submit, and the existing *review controls* check caught
  it as the workflow tax it was; Pre-flight shows the same gate.
  The record fields are read through `recFields().set` — the panel's own answer — and the two OGC
  fields are demanded off `destIsOgc()`, so a field that cannot apply is never demanded. Dropping
  the proposal *quietly* is the failure this replaced; withholding it by name is not dropping it.
- **Accepted the way `addReq()` adds**, at the bottom of the consolidated listing for the SFWG to
  place, with the office's ask values kept and every carried field set. Every write goes through
  `save()`, so it is in the change journal like any edit by hand.
- **Provenance stops the double-add.** The accepted requirement carries `from: {pmo, nid}`; the same
  submittal imported again shows *already added as NEW-n* and cannot be ticked.

The provisional id carries the office (`NEW-PSM-1`) so two offices can never mint the same one, and
the number is the **last** segment everywhere — `yearCopy` was reading `split('-')[1]`, which on
such an id is the office. Guarded by *a copy proposes a new requirement* (`audit-import.mjs`) and
the six `proposal-*` mutations.

### A bulk write lands only where the field is offered

`matApplyBulk()` wrote to every row `matRows()` returned. `matRows()` filters by office and by
the missing-field picker — never by whether the requirement *offers* the field. So a bulk
`MIPR type = Direct Cite` with no filter set landed on every prime, where the record panel never
shows it, and the value was real: in `S.mipr`, travelling in a submittal and a year copy, with
nothing on screen saying so. Pre-existing since `Which awards to` shipped.

**The obvious fix was wrong, and that is the lesson.** Skipping rows whose `recFields()` lacks the
label would have silently no-op'd the `↳ USAF` per-service budget setters, which are bulk-only
entries with no record label at all. So each `matBulk()` entry says for itself who it applies to
(`applies(it)`), and the default is everyone. The two gated fields are keyed on `destIsOgc()` —
**the same predicate the record panel uses to offer them**, written once, so the sweep and the
panel cannot disagree. One function, `matBulkRows()`, decides the reach for the preview, the
confirm and the write; a write that is scoped one way and previewed another is a dialog that
lies. The second-hop control is offered off that predicate too, for every non-prime kind.

Guarded by *a bulk write lands only where the field is offered* (`audit-board.mjs`), the
`bulk-lands-everywhere` and `second-hop-gov-only` mutations, and the register entry
`bulk-write-where-the-field-is-not-offered`.

### The data-loss fuzz — the other can't-fail

```
npm run fuzz                      # random seed, printed
node tools/roundtrip-fuzz.mjs --seed 424242     # replay exactly
npm run fuzz -- --rounds 6
```

The stated can't-fails on this programme are **data loss and math**. Three layers now attack the
math. This attacks the other one, and it attacks it the same way: not by checking a fixture someone
thought of, but by generating state nobody thought of and insisting it survives.

`audit-import.mjs` already proves save → reopen on a FIXED board. Necessary, not sufficient:
`year-drops-a-container` exists only because a person sat down and thought of the container it
drops. A fuzzer finds the container nobody thought about. Every container is populated with awkward
values — quotes, em dashes, a literal `</script>`, zero, a fractional cent, a 300-character string,
unicode — then driven through the trips the data actually takes:

| Trip | What it would mean to fail |
|---|---|
| **fill every container the board writes** | a container this tool never fills is a container whose loss it can never see — an empty one after `populate()` fails the run |
| save → reopen | a value written to the store is not there when the file is reopened |
| save → reopen → **save again** | it survives one trip and is re-encoded on the next, so it is lost on the third |
| year copy, every requirement | `yearCopy` created the container and never copied into it — checked for every container on `yearCopy`'s own normalise list, read from the board |
| PMO copy → edit → submit → take → review | a field an office changed on its copy is not in the package, or is not offered to the reviewer with the value the copy held — and a field it did NOT change is in the package anyway, which would overwrite the portfolio board's later edits on apply |

**Which containers exist is read from the board, never listed in the tool.** The first version
carried fourteen names under a comment saying *"if a field exists, it gets written here"*. By the
time anyone looked the board wrote fifty-two — the PMO reassignment overlay, the typed record
fields, the six CLIN containers, the accounting keys — and the fuzz had passed for days while a
value lost from any of them would have gone unnoticed. The `applyState()` normalise list is not the
source of truth either: `mipr`, `obCat`, `aceMap`, `whatif`, `upl`, `rcnAsg`, `budgetAsg` and every
`clin*` container are created lazily inside their own setters and never appear there. So the tool
scans `S.<key> =`, `S.<key>[…]`, `.push(` and `.splice(` out of the board's source, and a container
that is still empty after `populate()` fails the run by name. A container that genuinely cannot be
filled is excused in `EXCUSED` with its reason (today: only `pmoOrder`, rebuilt on every load), and
an excuse for a container the board no longer writes fails too, so that list cannot rot.

**The first run with every container filled found a shipped defect.** A board with buy lines seats
`BUY-AC` and `BUY-ENG` in `DATA.items` at boot; `BASE_ITEMS` is sliced right after, so the first save
wrote them into the data pack, and the next open rehydrated `DATA` from that pack and seated them
again: ×1, ×2, ×3 across three opens, measured on the board's own boot path. `S.order` held each id
once, so the priority list looked right; every surface that walks `DATA.items` would have counted
the buy lines twice, then three times. Registered as `buy-lines-duplicated-on-reopen`, guarded by
*buy lines reopen once* in `audit-import.mjs` and the `buy-lines-repushed` mutation.

The fuzz is a stage of `validate:math` on both builds. Its seed is random every run and printed.

**The seed is the whole point.** It is printed on every run and accepted with `--seed`, so a failure
at 3am is replayable at 9am with one command. `Math.random()` cannot be replayed, and a failure you
cannot reproduce is a rumour.

Proven to bite, on four injected defects: dropping `cp(T.mipr, …)` reports *"container mipr is
normalised on the target but never filled — 2 entries in the source year, 0 in the copy"*;
removing the load-bearing `.replace(/</g,'\\u003c')` reports exact paths —
`years.2027.alloc.R002  saved {"m":"t","v":0.01}  read undefined` — because a note containing
`</script>` closes the tag early and truncates the store; removing the `S.grp` write from
`populate()` reports *"1 container(s) the board writes are never filled by this fuzz — grp"*; and
making `pmoSubmitBuild()` skip `recNames` reports *"PRIME-000.recNames changed on the copy is not
what the package carries — copy held "0 edited", package undefined"*.

### The export delta — which numbers moved

```
npm run delta                         # working tree vs HEAD
node tools/export-delta.mjs --ref 6b21d1a
node tools/export-delta.mjs --ref <sha> --board prioritization-board-grid.html
```

*"Is the math right"* and *"what did this change move"* are **different questions**. Two versions can
both be internally consistent, both pass every suite, and still print different figures in the
workbook someone is about to brief. The suites answer the first. This answers the second, which is
the one asked in the room.

**No baseline file.** It builds every export twice — once from the working tree, once from a git ref
— and diffs. A stored baseline is a file someone has to remember to refresh, and a stale one reports
movement that already happened. Git already knows what the board used to be. The fixture is FIXED in
both runs, so every difference belongs to the code.

It always exits 0: movement is information, not a fault. Figures move for good reasons. The question
is whether the ones that moved are the ones you meant to move.

> **Two addressing bugs found while building it, both of which produced PHANTOM movement.** Decision
> Bands carries TWO header blocks — a summary and the detail table — and addressing detail rows
> against the summary's header put every column past it on `col#30`, which then read as *"Funded %
> moved by $14.4M"* the moment a column was inserted. And keying a row by column 0 collided every
> Spend Plan Source row onto `2027`, because column 0 there is the fiscal year. Fixed by taking the
> **nearest header above each row** and building the row key from its leading text cells with a
> disambiguating counter. Phantom movement is the one thing this tool must not produce: a report
> that cries wolf gets skimmed, and then a real move goes past unread. Before the fix it reported 15
> moved figures against v1.57; after, 3 — and those 3 are the real Spend Plan Source basis change.

### The nightly math validation, and the one thing the suites cannot tell you

```
npm run validate:math        # the whole arithmetic gate, ~130 min (two sweeps of 250 mutations side by side)
npm run coverage:money       # just the coverage census, ~1 s
```

A Routine fires `validate:math` every night at **07:00 UTC** (about 3am US Eastern) in a fresh
session, which clones `main`, runs the gate and reports. It is explicitly forbidden from committing,
pushing or "fixing" anything: a change made unattended at 3am to a board people brief from is not
worth the convenience. It reports, and a person decides.

The orchestrator runs **every stage even when an earlier one fails** — a nightly report that stops at
the first red tells you one thing and hides five — and exits non-zero if any stage failed. Stages:
the defect register, money coverage (both builds), the trace matrix (both builds), the 7 suites
(both builds), parity, the browser checks, and both mutation sweeps in parallel.

### What the run costs, and why the suite order is a cost decision

**~67 minutes**, of which the two mutation sweeps are ~62 and ~64 (they run in parallel, so the
wall clock is the slower one). Everything else totals about three minutes.

The sweep is 250 mutations × up to 6 suites × 2 builds, and the loop **short-circuits on the first
suite that catches**. That makes the order of `SUITES` a performance decision, not a stylistic one:
a suite placed early is paid for by every mutation the ones before it miss. Measured on an idle box:

| suite | cost |
|---|---|
| `audit-other` | 2.9s |
| `audit-ppt` | 3.7s |
| `audit-recon` | 4.2s |
| `audit` (excel) | 8.2s |
| `audit-board` | 12.4s |
| `audit-import` | **23.8s** |

`audit-import` is four times the average and catches the narrowest slice — every suite above it
watches the way *out* of the board — so it goes **last**. It used to sit ahead of `recon`, which
meant every mutation `recon` caught paid 23.8 seconds for nothing first.

**Mutations run concurrently; the suites inside one mutation do not.** `MUT_JOBS` defaults to half
the cores, because `validate:math` runs two sweeps side by side — so two sweeps × two workers
saturates a four-core box. Parallelising the *suites* would be wrong: the loop stops at the first
catch, so it would pay for work it does not need.

> **The 11 equivalent mutations run all six suites every night — about ten minutes — and can never
> be caught.** Skipping them is the obvious saving and it is deliberately not taken: running them is
> the only way to detect an equivalence that has gone **stale**. An equivalence is a claim that a
> line cannot be broken; the night it stops being true is the night you need to hear about it.

### The defect register — every math error this board has shipped

`math-defects.json`, checked by `npm run defects`, is the first stage of the nightly run. **When a
math defect is found, it gets an entry.** Not as a changelog: every entry names the CHECK that would
now catch it and the MUTATIONS that prove that check still bites, and the stage FAILS if one of
those mutation ids has disappeared from `mutate.mjs`.

That is the only property worth having. *"We fixed the phantom UFR"* is a claim. *"The mutation that
reintroduces it is still in the sweep and the sweep still catches it"* is a measurement. A register
written in prose rots — the entries stay while the checks that earned them get refactored away, and
a year later it is a list of things we used to test.

Write the **symptom as the person saw it**, not as the code explains it. *"A service showed fully
funded beside its own UFR"* is what gets recognised again next time; *"`tiers()` caps `dd` at
`dRem[i]`"* is not. The register is read by whoever is staring at a number that looks wrong.

It does **not** re-run the mutations — the sweep does that, and doing it twice would double a
hour-long run. This stage asserts the guards *exist and are wired in*; the sweep proves they *bite*.
Together they mean a fixed defect cannot come back unnoticed.

`guarded: false` is outstanding debt — a defect fixed but never pinned down by a mutation. It is
reported every night and does not fail the run, the same posture as a coverage gap: record the
truth, gate on regression.

**`npm run coverage:money` is the new one, and it answers a question no suite can.** Every suite can
be green while a surface has no check at all: the Spend Plan Source shipped with its Amount column
asserted by nothing, and changing its basis moved a fixture total from 285 to 150 with all twelve
suites passing. The detector is the mutation sweep itself — a money surface with no mutation aimed
at it is a surface nothing is proving anything about — so it maps every mutation's search string to
the function body containing it and reports any money surface with none.

It refuses to go stale in three directions, each of which is a trap this codebase has already fallen
into once:

| It fails when | Because |
|---|---|
| a surface exists in the board but not in the REGISTRY | a new export must not appear without someone saying whether it emits money |
| the REGISTRY names a function the board no longer has | a renamed export must not leave a dead promise of coverage behind it |
| a recorded `gap` now HAS coverage | debt that was paid must be struck off, or the list of known holes becomes fiction |

That last one fired on its first run: `execSynopsis` was recorded as a gap and already had three
mutations.

> **A sixth way a check can look like it works: a fixture that cannot reach the code it aims at.**
> The whole-dollar scenario put its legacy-split requirement LAST in the priority order. The first
> requirement exhausted every discretionary lane, so the split's fractional shares were capped at a
> dry lane's zero and never reached the output — the check passed, proving nothing, and
> `split-share-fractional` survived the sweep and said so. Walking it FIRST caught the mutation
> immediately. This is why a new check is not finished until a mutation has survived against it
> once: a green check is evidence of nothing until something has made it go red.
>
> **It happened twice the same day.** The trim-absorption check asserts that the remaining lane is
> offered top-down by PRIORITY, not by size — and the fixture made its first requirement both the
> highest priority and the largest ask, so the two orders produced the same answer and
> `trim-absorb-unordered` survived. A fixture that cannot tell two rules apart proves neither.
> When a check defends an ORDER, a RULE or a CHOICE, the fixture has to make the alternatives
> DISAGREE, or the check is a tautology wearing a scenario.

**A gap is debt, not a pass.** Five money surfaces have no mutation aimed at them today — `crData`,
`exportFlow`, `exportCoa`, `exportReprog`, `exportAceTieout` — each recorded with a date and what it
would take. They do not fail the run; they are printed every night until they are paid down, and the
run DOES fail the moment a new one appears. This is the same posture as the colour baseline: record
the truth, gate on regression, never assert a standard the thing was not built to meet.

> **Watch the gate itself.** `audit:suites` chains SEVEN suites but only SIX print
> `ALL CHECKS PASSED` — `audit:legacy` closes with its own sentence, which is why 12 banners across
> both builds is healthy and 14 is not a target. The first version of this orchestrator gated on 7
> and failed on a perfectly good board. A nightly job that cries wolf is one nobody reads, so the
> gate is the **exit code**, with the banner count reported as a fact and the legacy line asserted
> separately so that suite cannot go quiet unnoticed.

### The sweep runs on Brad's named go (20 Oct 2026)

**During development `tools/export-audit/mutate.mjs` is TEXT ONLY.** Build one mutant with `recovery/v200/bin/mkmut.py
<mutate.mjs> <board> <id> <out.html>` and count every target with `recovery/v200/bin/mcount.py <mutate.mjs> <board>` (both
`python3 -I`; they read the array as text and run nothing from it). Prove a new check by running its block on the board and on
its own mutant. Never `import` `mutate.mjs` (loading it starts the sweep), and never run `npm run mutate` or `validate:math`.

**A sweep runs only when Brad names one**, and then: on a committed tree that does not change while it runs; in saved batches
that survive a container restart (v1.99's batch runner -- four single six-hour runs were each lost to a restart); with nothing
else running; and it reports and changes nothing. The rule is about cost and control, not safety: at 2,021 targets a full sweep
takes most of a day on this container, and an unattended one started mid-work has cost whole runs before.

Why not drop the sweep: about 880 targets added in v2.00 and v2.01 have been proven only one at a time, against their own check,
never as a set. Brad's plan (20 Oct): once v2.01 is on `main`, sweep those first; a full sweep before the hosted rebuild.

**Show only what changed (Brad, 21 Oct 2026).** When a sweep runs, and whenever a mutation or any other modification is
applied, report only the specific lines that changed, or a unified diff block. Never rewrite the unchanged parts of a
file or reprint a whole script. A mutant is its id, its find string and its replacement (what `mkmut.py` prints), never
the board; a suite or tool edit is its diff.

**The nightly Routine runs every stage but the sweep**: `npm run validate:nightly` (`validate-math.mjs --no-sweep`), whose pass line
says the sweep was not run, so a nightly pass is never read as one that proved the checks bite. The Routine ("Nightly ORBIT math
validation (no sweep)", 07:00 UTC) was rewritten on 20 Oct: the night before, it ended its turn 44 seconds into a full sweep. The
sections below describe how the sweep works and what it has found; read their commands through this rule.

**The sharded sweep** (`tools/export-audit/sweep-shards.mjs`, 20 Oct 2026) asks mutate.mjs's question with the two long suites cut
into pieces. Why: mutate.mjs runs whole suites, and audit-recon and audit-import run dozens of blocks in one process whose memory
adds up -- a 20-target pilot of v2.00/v2.01 targets took 86 min for 19 and 6 of them died CRASHED, out of heap under the 2.5 GB cap.
Calibration found the cause: recon's block 80 alone peaks at 6.5 GB RSS (it passes only at an 8 GB heap), and import's blocks 105
and 107-108 need 5 GB. How it works:
- **Cutting.** A shard of recon or import is the suite with every block it does not keep REMOVED (`await (async () => {`…`})();`
  and recon's bare `{`…`}`); prelude, helpers between blocks, the self-check and the summary stay, so the "never ran" check sees only
  the kept blocks' names. A block reading a top-level `let` another writes (V185W, V186L, V14) takes its writer along. Every other
  suite, legacy-fsrc included, runs whole. Shard files are `_shard-<suite>-NNN.mjs` beside the suites and are deleted at exit.
- **Calibration, the safety net.** Every piece is run on the CLEAN board first; one that fails a check there would read every mutant
  as caught, so it is merged with its predecessor (or the suite runs whole) and never used alone. One that DIES (no banner) is rerun at
  5 GB, then 8 GB, and is given in the sweep the heap it passed at. Passing pieces are packed to about 90 s and each pack is proved on
  the clean board again. Results go to `<out>/calibration.json`, reused while the board and every file in `tools/export-audit/` hash
  the same; each clean run is kept in `cal-runs.jsonl` as it ends, so a cut-off calibration resumes. Measured: 18 min, 38 shards
  (recon 11, import 19, eight whole suites); all 38 one after another take 40 min.
- **Verdict = mutate.mjs's.** Caught = exit 1 with a failing-check banner; a death is retried once at twice the heap; a shard that dies
  twice makes CRASHED only if no other shard catches. Every shard is tried before SURVIVED; `equivalent:` mutations report equivalent.
- **Order is cost only**: v200-* tries audit-inject first, then shards carrying the id's workstream tag (U44, R5), the mutation's
  area, the rarer words of its id, then the rest by calibrated time. Ids come from `mut-json.py` (mutate.mjs read as text).
- **Proof (20 Oct):** the 20-target pilot, all 20 caught -- the 13 the old sweep caught, the 6 it CRASHED and the one it never
  reached; a no-op control (find = repl) SURVIVED after all 38 shards; `abs-room-unfloored` came back equivalent. **Speed, honestly:**
  89 min wall for those 22 at 4 jobs, a mean of ~15 min per target per job (12 min for the 20; ~40 min for a target that must try every
  shard). That is not faster than the old sweep per target; what it buys is a verdict where the old one crashed. The ordering by tag
  and prefix was added after that run and has not been measured; it cannot change a verdict.

```
node tools/export-audit/sweep-shards.mjs --ids FILE [--extra controls.json] [--out DIR] [--jobs N] [--budget MB] [--calibrate-only]
```
Resumable: `<out>/results.jsonl` gets one line per mutant and a restart skips those ids.

### Measuring the suites — run this after adding checks

```
npm run mutate            # all mutations
npm run mutate -- mc      # just the Mission Critical ones
```

Green suites prove nothing on their own: a check that never runs looks exactly like a passing one.
`mutate.mjs` breaks one line of export math at a time and requires a suite to fail. A mutation whose
search string no longer matches is reported **NOT APPLIED**, never as caught — a stale target must
not masquerade as coverage.

**NEVER EDIT A SUITE WHILE A SWEEP IS RUNNING.** `mutate.mjs` reads the board source ONCE at module
load, so editing `prioritization-board*.html` mid-sweep is safe — and that half of the rule is what
makes the other half so easy to get wrong. The SUITES are not read once: line 645 spawns each one
with `execFileSync(process.execPath, [path], …)`, a **fresh process per mutation**, so a suite file
edited at mutation 92 is the file every later mutation runs.

That is not merely stale, it inverts the result. `mutate.mjs` treats **any** non-zero exit as
"caught" and then `break`s. A suite that fails unconditionally against the swept board — because it
now asserts behaviour that board does not have yet — therefore marks every mutation the earlier
suites missed as `caught (board)`, and `import`, `recon` and `legacy` never run at all. The sweep
reports **zero survivors** and means nothing by it. This happened: MIPR checks were added to
`audit-board.mjs` while a sweep of a pre-MIPR board was at 92/153, and the run had to be thrown away.

So: a sweep pins the board, not the harness. Commit first, sweep the committed tree, and leave
`tools/` alone until it finishes. If a suite must change, kill the sweep and start it again — a
sweep you have to reason about is worth less than one you re-ran.

Every surviving mutation is a line nothing protects. Add the check, do not delete the mutation.
Four kinds of gap it has already found, none of which the checks alone would have shown:

- **Conservation is not enough.** `d + m + u == basis` still holds when a draw is merely mis-split —
  short-change the discretionary draw and the shortfall moves into mandatory, every total ties. The
  suite now re-derives the greedy walk independently (the "lane oracle") and compares draw for draw.
- **A check that cannot run reads as a pass.** Assertions matched on a title the slide never renders,
  and a figure was computed but never asserted. Both looked green for as long as they existed.
- **AN ORACLE THAT ASKS THE CODE UNDER TEST WILL AGREE WITH IT.** The lane oracle obtained the
  allocation basis by calling `svcAlloc()` in the page, so a mutation splitting a trimmed allocation
  evenly across services — instead of weighting it by each service's ask — passed every suite. The
  sheets faithfully printed the wrong split and the oracle confirmed it. `basisOf()` in `audit.mjs`
  now restates the rule from the scenario's own inputs and never reads it back from the page.
  **Derive expectations from the fixture, never from the code you are testing.**
- **A path with no fixture has no coverage, however many checks point at it.** Every scenario passed
  a per-service allocation vector (`m:'s'`), so the scalar-trim branch of `svcAlloc` — one figure
  typed into the row's allocation box, the commonest edit on the board — was never once executed.
  Likewise every funding-source override sat on a requirement with no allocation, making "basis on
  the full ask" and "basis on the allocation" indistinguishable. Both now have scenarios.
- **A figure the board shows but never exports is invisible to export suites.** The cumulative could
  be made to skip unfunded dollars entirely with all three export suites green, because no export
  carries it — and it is what places the cutline banners. Sweeping the scoreboard tiles and the
  discretionary fit strip afterwards found **every one of their figures equally unprotected**,
  including the PROGRAM FLOOR, which is one of the two stated Mission Critical rules. `audit-board.mjs`
  covers the board's own derived figures; add to it rather than stretching an export suite.
- **Read the rendered page, do not recompute.** `audit-board.mjs` pulls each tile's figure out of the
  DOM. Re-evaluating the same expression in the harness would compare a copy against a copy and pass
  whatever the board did — the lane-oracle trap in a different costume. A pattern that no longer
  matches is recorded as a FAILURE, never skipped.
- **`typeof x === 'function'` on an out-of-scope name fails OPEN, silently.** The grid inspector read
  `★ MC shortage` as `(typeof mcShortOf === 'function') ? mcShortOf(it) : 0`. `mcShortOf` is a
  `const` **inside `exportExcel()`**, so it is not in scope there — and `typeof` on an undeclared
  identifier returns `'undefined'` instead of throwing, so the guard swallowed it and the field read
  0 for every requirement. Every starred row said "covered", however short it was, for as long as
  that line existed. A guard that turns a missing dependency into a plausible default hides the
  failure it was written to reveal; call the global helper and let a genuine mistake throw.
  It survived because `inspector-agrees.mjs` compared requirement, discretionary, mandatory and
  unfunded — but never the MC field. **A rendered figure with no assertion is decoration.**
- **A scenario absent is a rule untested.** No board scenario had a starred requirement, so all four
  Mission Critical tile figures were being compared against zero and agreed. Adding MC scenarios also
  exposed a bug in the harness's own tier oracle, which had missed the line that makes a must-pay read
  `mc` only once mandatory or unfunded touches it.
- **"Nothing else moved" has to be checked on everything, not on one example.** The view toggle's
  scope check asserted that Unfunded Requirements was unaffected — and a mutation that leaked the
  view into the shared truth walk survived it, because that sheet's rows are computed *before* the
  month sheets and the leak could only corrupt what came after. The check was guarding the one sheet
  the bug could not reach. It now compares **every sheet** between the two views. When a rule says
  "and nothing else changes", enumerate the everything; a representative sample picks itself for
  the wrong reasons.
- **A string check in a numeric harness fails silently in the wrong direction.** `chk()` compared with
  `Math.abs(got - want) <= EPS`; handed two equal strings that is `NaN <= EPS`, i.e. false, so every
  marker assertion "failed" and then crashed the reporter on `got.toFixed`. The crash was the lucky
  part — had `chk` returned a pass for non-numbers instead, the marker checks would have read green
  forever. Widen the comparator with the assertion, not after it.

**Equivalent mutations.** Two are marked `equivalent:` rather than surviving: flooring the lane
remainders cannot change any output, because every draw is clamped by `Math.max(0, dRem[i])` and
nothing can drive a remainder negative. That is a claim with a receipt — `audit-board.mjs` asserts
the clamping invariants, so if a clamp is ever removed the mutation stops being equivalent and the
suite fails. Mark a mutation equivalent only with that kind of backing; a survivor that is merely
inconvenient is still a survivor.

## Never commit a populated board

The repo holds the **program**. A working board is program and data fused into one file (see
below), and that data is budget detail that does not belong on GitHub. The committed copy must
always have `pubState` as `null` and `APPNS` empty — `check.mjs` enforces both.

To work on a change against real data, describe the data shape rather than uploading the board.

## How it saves — read this before changing anything structural

The file is its own database.

- `SRC0` is captured at boot as `'<!DOCTYPE html>\n' + document.documentElement.outerHTML`.
- `buildSavedHtml()` takes `SRC0` and **regex-replaces the `pubState` script tag** with the current
  state as JSON -- through `savedHtmlOf(appns, cycDb)` (v1.95), the one assembly every board file this page
  writes goes through: 💾 Save, the PMO copy, the work kept aside, and the board as it was before a full
  import that could not be put back.
- `saveInPlace()` writes that back over the file itself via the File System Access API
  (`showSaveFilePicker`), caching the handle in IndexedDB (`prio-fh`), with a collision guard that asks
  whenever the file on disk is any save other than the one the work on screen came from (`LASTKNOWN`,
  v1.89 -- see that section: it compared against the file's own time, and a reopen made that wrong).
  A file it cannot read, or that is not a board, is never written over. Since v1.95: one save at a time
  (`saveSerial`, and the Web Lock `'orbit-save'` across tabs), each acknowledging exactly the bytes it
  wrote (`_saveGen`), and one writer per board file (`WRITER`) -- see the v1.95 section.

So: **the `pubState` tag's exact literal shape is load-bearing.** Reformat it and `Save` silently
writes a file containing the *old* state. `check.mjs` guards this.

`localStorage` is a convenience cache only — the file is the record. Keys: `prio27om`, `prio27ff`,
`prio27sp`, `prio27opn`, `prio27execdb`, `prio27acedb`, `prio27reprog` (the reprogramming log: carried, not shown, since
v2.02), `prio27appn`, `prio27who`,
`spmtTheme`, `spmtSidecar`, `prio27cycGo` (v2.05: the appropriation a Go to under PPBE cycles is opening, removed when it opens); and, per appropriation key, since v1.89: `<key>.base` (which saved copy
the work came from, and whether this browser holds work no saved copy has) and `<key>.kept` (work kept
aside when a newer saved copy replaced it). Neither is ever part of the board. Since v1.95:
`prio27writerKey` (this browser's writer key; the file holds only its fingerprint) and `prio27impMixed`
(a full import stopped part-way and could not be put back: nothing writes over the board file until it is
cleared). Since v1.90: `spmtMode`
(the workspace mode), `spmtDensity` and `spmtDetail` (Funding detail open) -- this browser's view of the
board, like `spmtTheme`, never written into the file (`tools/layout-fits.mjs` G checks that). Since v2.09, in a PMO copy:
every side list and the stopped-import hold are sealed with the copy's publish stamp (`prio27execdb-pmo<ts>`, `prio27acedb-`,
`prio27buddb-`, `prio27reprog-`, `prio27sideOpen-`, `prio27impMixed-pmo<ts>`), as its plan, cycles and destinations already
were; the copy's own file handle is kept in `prio-fh` under `pmo-copy|<publish stamp>|<office>`, never the board's `FNAME`
entry; and the copy's plan carries `MS.pmoFor`, the office its file was saved for (the board drops it on every load).

## Hand-edit points

These were templated per board by the build script. Line numbers are as of `v1.31`; find them
by symbol name, not by number.

| What | Symbol | ~Line |
|---|---|---|
| Version stamp, shown in the header and in transport handshakes | `BUILD` | 1086 |
| Save filename | `FNAME` | 4029 |
| Fiscal year | in `document.title`, `#fyTag`, `_AP.exp` strings | 535, 981 |
| Which appropriations exist | `APPN_SLUGS` | 897 |

**Bump `BUILD` on every functional change.** It is the stamp shown in the header, in the transport
panels, and in the "written by a newer version" warning an older copy shows when it refuses to read
a newer file — a stale stamp makes that diagnosis lie. Note that the *compatibility gate* is a
separate integer, `LIVE_VER` (currently 1), compared by `liveTooNew()`. Raise `LIVE_VER` only when
the shared-state wire format changes in a way older copies must refuse rather than misread.

### Adding or removing an appropriation

Six tables must change together. The file's own comment at `APPN_SLUGS` explains why this is
dangerous: a slug missed in one table does not throw. It quietly covers one appropriation fewer — a
chip row missing a button, an export summing three of four — and every figure it still prints
reconciles, so nothing looks wrong. `check.mjs` is the replacement for the suite check that used to
hold this.

Keyed by slug: `APPN_SLUGS`, `APPN_LBL`, `APPN_NAME`, `_SLUGMAP`, `SRC_TABS`.
Keyed by SHOUTING label: `APPNS`, `_AP`.

`APPN_SLUGS` must stay in the first module — `FLOW_APPNS` reads it while the script is still
evaluating, and a `const` declared below its reader is a dead-zone throw that kills the page.

| Region | Lines | Contents |
|---|---|---|
| CSS | 6–545 | Five themes as CSS token sets: default, `navy`, `parchment`, `blueprint`, `harbor` |
| Theme bootstrap | 519–530 | Applies the saved theme before the body paints, to avoid a flash |
| Body | 532–878 | 153 IDs — header, toolbars, panels, drawers |
| `pubState` | 878 | Saved state. `null` in this repo, always |
| Main script | 879–19483 | The application |

Inside the main script, roughly in order: appropriation tables and boot (879–1100), funding
realignment (1600), FYDP (3247), change journal (3794), appropriation packages (4229), RCN/EEIC
(5418), CR cash view (5790), a from-scratch XLSX reader (5984), budget families (6454), pre-flight
(7009), WBS from execution data (7342), ACE mapping (7468), CCaR reconciliation (7813), funds-flow
charts (8216), decision bands (10321; the executive decision tool there was removed in v2.05), QPE coverage (10735), spend plans (11046), PPBE
baselines (11716), dated snapshots (12618), reprogramming tracker (13708), CLIN drill (14312), WBS
(14501), XLSX writer (15412; the PPTX export after it was removed in v2.05), Live View (16311),
shared-folder transport (16472–17361; Confluence, SharePoint and the JSON endpoint removed in v2.04), companion tools (17774),
ACE tie-out workbook (18213).

The minimal XLSX writer (§36) carries its own stylesheet. The 1.62 MB PPTX template that sat after it (the SFWG/SFCB
membership deck the PowerPoint export was built inside) was removed with the deck in v2.05, item 114.

The XLSX reader uses the browser's native `DecompressionStream`; there is no zip library.

## Section 508 / WCAG 2.2 — the one outside standard

`npm run standards` (`tools/standards-check.mjs`) measures the board against 36 CFR 1194, which
